English for Work · Health & life sciences

Home Care & Caregiving English

Support choice and dignity during home visits, report observations precisely, and coordinate dependable care without exceeding your role.

8 practical lessonsIntermediate to advanced1 complete learner book

Make yourself clear.

For Home care aides, personal care assistants, and caregivers.

Copy complete AI prompts ↓

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Clarify scheduled support, acknowledge an unmet expectation, and request authorization without promising an unassigned service.
  • Offer support without pressure, respect a changed preference, and record the agreed timing without judgment.
  • Attribute the client's words, describe a change from usual behavior, and seek the appropriate response without diagnosing.
  • Explain a reminder-only role, acknowledge a medication question, and help obtain qualified advice without recommending a dose.
  • Acknowledge a food preference, explain an unresolved restriction, and offer an approved alternative without pressure.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

People, schedules, care plans, and incidents are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not clinical care, medication instructions, capacity assessment, or professional certification. Follow the actual care plan, consent and privacy requirements, training, employer procedures, and qualified clinical advice. For urgent or emergency concerns use the appropriate local service and procedure without waiting for a routine callback. The cited England/UK sources do not establish universal rules.

01Confirming the visit and the care planThe appointment is real; the transport is unconfirmed+

Your goal

Clarify scheduled support, acknowledge an unmet expectation, and request authorization without promising an unassigned service.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

The appointment is real; the transport is unconfirmed

Caregiver Maya arrives at 9:00 for a two-hour visit with Mr. Ellis. Her assignment lists breakfast support and light household tasks. He expects her to drive him to a 10:00 appointment, but transport is not listed and she has no authorization to provide it. The coordinator is available by phone. Maya must establish the discrepancy, contact the coordinator with the client involved as appropriate, and continue agreed support within her role. Neither a phone message nor a request for help confirms a transport booking.

Mr. Ellis: Client / Maya: Caregiver

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. When is the scheduled visit due to end?

Answer and explanation

B. 11:00

Two hours from the stated 9:00 arrival ends at 11:00.

2. What is not authorized in the supplied assignment?

Answer and explanation

D. Transport to the appointment

Transport is absent from the assignment and Maya has no transport authorization.

3. What should Maya clarify with the coordinator?

Answer and explanation

A. The transport expectation and an authorized arrangement

The coordinator can check the discrepancy and appropriate arrangement without Maya inventing authorization.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

An appointment time is not a transport booking. A transport request is not authorization for the caregiver to drive. Keep the appointment, departure arrangements, transport provider, and booking status distinct when asking the coordinator to resolve the discrepancy.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
care plan
A record of assessed needs and agreed support arrangements.follow the care plan
assignment
The particular work allocated to a worker.confirm the assignment
visit window
The specified period for an expected visit.confirm the visit window
scheduled duration
The planned length of a visit or service.check the scheduled duration
care coordinator
The person coordinating relevant care arrangements.contact the care coordinator
support need
An area in which a person requires assistance.clarify a support need
ADLs
Activities of daily living, such as dressing or eating.support ADLs
IADLs
Instrumental activities of daily living, such as shopping or household management.assist with IADLs
personal care
Support with personal daily activities within an agreed role.provide personal care
domestic support
Agreed help with household activities.arrange domestic support
light housekeeping
Limited household tasks within the assigned service.clarify light housekeeping
appointment
A planned meeting or service at a specified time.confirm the appointment
transport authorization
Permission for the particular transport arrangement.obtain transport authorization
escort
A person accompanying someone under an agreed arrangement.arrange an escort
scope of duties
The tasks permitted within the worker's actual role.clarify the scope of duties
service discrepancy
A difference between expected and recorded service.report a service discrepancy
unmet expectation
Something expected that has not been confirmed or provided.acknowledge an unmet expectation
booking confirmation
Evidence that a particular service reservation is accepted.obtain booking confirmation
accessible transport
Transport suited to the person's relevant access needs.request accessible transport
contingency arrangement
A prepared alternative for a possible disruption.activate a contingency arrangement
contact route
The approved way to reach the appropriate person.use the contact route
visit record
The factual account of support and events during a visit.update the visit record
authorized change
A change agreed through the required process.document an authorized change
read-back
Repeating key information to check that it was understood correctly.give a read-back

The care plan describes agreed support; the assignment identifies this visit and worker. If the two appear inconsistent, use the approved clarification process. Do not silently expand duties, erase the request, or record an unconfirmed service as arranged.

C Notice the language

A boundary needs a useful next step

An unexpected limit can sound like abandonment. Acknowledge the practical consequence, explain the assignment plainly, and identify who can clarify the arrangement. Avoid blaming the client for expecting something that may have been discussed elsewhere.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Introduce the visit
Good morning, Mr. Ellis. I am here for your nine-to-eleven visit.
Confirm the plan
My assignment lists breakfast support and light household tasks.
Invite clarification
What were you expecting for your appointment this morning?
Acknowledge the impact
I understand that getting there at ten matters to you.
Name the discrepancy
Transport is not listed in the assignment I have.
Explain the boundary
I do not have authorization to drive you.
Offer a next step
I can contact the coordinator now to check the arrangement.
Ask to include the client
Would you like to be part of that conversation?
Report without blame
There is a difference between the expected support and my recorded assignment.
Check the status
Has transport actually been booked, or only requested?
Clarify responsibility
Who is confirming the transport arrangement?
Check access needs
Please confirm any access requirements through the agreed process.
Avoid a false promise
I cannot confirm a driver until the coordinator verifies the booking.
Continue agreed support
While we clarify that, would you like help with breakfast?
Repeat key details
Let me check: the appointment is at ten, and transport is still unconfirmed.
Record the outcome
I will record the request, who was contacted, and the confirmed response.
6 language and precision notes
My assignment lists
Refers to the available record without accusing another person of being wrong.
I understand that
Recognizes the practical impact before explaining a limit.
Do not have authorization
Describes a specific boundary, not a personal unwillingness to help.
Has actually been booked
Separates a completed arrangement from an earlier request.
While we clarify that
Connects ongoing agreed support with the unresolved issue.
Still unconfirmed
Preserves uncertainty rather than implying that transport cannot be arranged.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement is both helpful and accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. Transport is not in my assignment; I can call the coordinator to check it now.

The correct response identifies the boundary and supplies an appropriate immediate clarification route.

2. Which record would overstate progress?

Answer and explanation

A. Transport confirmed, when only a message has been sent

Sending a request does not establish that a provider accepted or confirmed transport.

3. What does a read-back help establish?

Answer and explanation

A. Shared understanding of the appointment and transport status

Repeating key details checks communication but does not supply missing service authorization.

4. Which question is most useful?

Answer and explanation

A. Who will confirm the arrangement and contact Mr. Ellis?

Identifying the responsible contact makes the unresolved arrangement actionable without inventing a provider.

E Conversations

The appointment is real; the transport is unconfirmed

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Mr. Ellis: Client / Maya: Caregiver

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • care coordinator
  • service discrepancy
  • booking confirmation
  • contact route
  • scope of duties
  • assignment
  • transport authorization
  • visit record
  • read-back
  • unmet expectation
  1. Mr. Ellis
    Good morning, Maya. My appointment is at ten, so I thought we would have breakfast quickly and then leave in your car.
  2. Maya
    Good morning. My 1 is for breakfast support and light household tasks from nine until eleven. I do not have transport listed, but I can check that with the coordinator now.
  3. Mr. Ellis
    I mentioned the appointment when someone called last week. I understood that the visit would include getting me there, not just making breakfast.
  4. Maya
    I understand the 2. We should clarify what was discussed without assuming you misunderstood. Getting to the appointment matters, and I want the coordinator to check the arrangement directly.
  5. Mr. Ellis
    Could you take me this once? It is only a short drive, and I do not want to lose the appointment.
  6. Maya
    I do not have 3. I can help contact the person who can resolve the request, but I cannot make a private driving arrangement outside my assigned role.
  7. Mr. Ellis
    All right. Please explain that the appointment is at ten. I would rather speak to the coordinator with you than repeat everything later.
  8. Maya
    Of course. We can give a clear 4 of the details together: appointment at ten, visit from nine to eleven, and no confirmed transport information in my assignment.
  9. Mr. Ellis
    Does that mean nobody has booked a car? There might be something in the office records that neither of us can see.
  10. Maya
    Exactly. The 5 may be elsewhere. I will ask what is actually arranged rather than tell you that nothing exists when I have not checked.
  11. Mr. Ellis
    Ten is when I need to be at the appointment, not when a car should collect me. Please make that clear when we call.
  12. Maya
    I will explain that distinction to the 6. The appointment time and pickup time are separate, and any access arrangements need to be confirmed through the proper process.
  13. Mr. Ellis
    What happens to breakfast while we are sorting this out? I have not eaten yet, and I still want the agreed help.
  14. Maya
    Breakfast remains part of my 7. We can continue that support with your agreement while the office checks the transport, without marking the additional request as settled.
  15. Mr. Ellis
    Please keep me on the call. Last time, I heard someone was dealing with it but never got a pickup time or a driver's name.
  16. Maya
    We will ask for the responsible person and the 8 for the response. If an arrangement changes, we need the actual details, not just an assurance that the request was passed on.
  17. Mr. Ellis
    Thank you. I can explain what I was told last week, and you can explain what is on your assignment this morning.
  18. Maya
    That will help us report the 9 fairly. I will keep your account separate from what the current record says, so the coordinator can reconcile them.
  19. Mr. Ellis
    Please put the outcome in the notes as well. The next person should not have to start the same conversation from the beginning.
  20. Maya
    I will update the 10 with the request, contact, and confirmed response. Until we have that response, I will describe transport as unconfirmed rather than booked.

Answers and explanations
  1. assignment Turn 2. The assignment identifies Maya's allocated tasks; it does not include permission to provide transport.
  2. unmet expectation Turn 4. The expected transport has not been confirmed in the available assignment, and the impact deserves acknowledgment.
  3. transport authorization Turn 6. Transport authorization is missing; a short journey or urgent request does not supply it.
  4. read-back Turn 8. A read-back checks shared understanding of the time and unresolved arrangement without creating a booking.
  5. booking confirmation Turn 10. Booking confirmation would establish an accepted reservation; the current absence of information does not prove no booking exists.
  6. care coordinator Turn 12. The care coordinator is the available role responsible for clarifying relevant arrangements, not a presumed transport provider.
  7. scope of duties Turn 14. The scope of duties includes breakfast support while the separate transport request remains unresolved.
  8. contact route Turn 16. The contact route identifies how the responsible person will communicate a confirmed response or further update.
  9. service discrepancy Turn 18. The service discrepancy is the difference between expected transport and the available assignment, not an established finding of fault.
  10. visit record Turn 20. The visit record should preserve the request, contacts, and actual outcome without converting pending work into completion.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Mr. Ellis: Good morning, Maya. My appointment is at ten, so I thought we would have breakfast quickly and then leave in your car.
  2. Maya: Good morning. My assignment is for breakfast support and light household tasks from nine until eleven. I do not have transport listed, but I can check that with the coordinator now.
  3. Mr. Ellis: I mentioned the appointment when someone called last week. I understood that the visit would include getting me there, not just making breakfast.
  4. Maya: I understand the unmet expectation. We should clarify what was discussed without assuming you misunderstood. Getting to the appointment matters, and I want the coordinator to check the arrangement directly.
  5. Mr. Ellis: Could you take me this once? It is only a short drive, and I do not want to lose the appointment.
  6. Maya: I do not have transport authorization. I can help contact the person who can resolve the request, but I cannot make a private driving arrangement outside my assigned role.
  7. Mr. Ellis: All right. Please explain that the appointment is at ten. I would rather speak to the coordinator with you than repeat everything later.
  8. Maya: Of course. We can give a clear read-back of the details together: appointment at ten, visit from nine to eleven, and no confirmed transport information in my assignment.
  9. Mr. Ellis: Does that mean nobody has booked a car? There might be something in the office records that neither of us can see.
  10. Maya: Exactly. The booking confirmation may be elsewhere. I will ask what is actually arranged rather than tell you that nothing exists when I have not checked.
  11. Mr. Ellis: Ten is when I need to be at the appointment, not when a car should collect me. Please make that clear when we call.
  12. Maya: I will explain that distinction to the care coordinator. The appointment time and pickup time are separate, and any access arrangements need to be confirmed through the proper process.
  13. Mr. Ellis: What happens to breakfast while we are sorting this out? I have not eaten yet, and I still want the agreed help.
  14. Maya: Breakfast remains part of my scope of duties. We can continue that support with your agreement while the office checks the transport, without marking the additional request as settled.
  15. Mr. Ellis: Please keep me on the call. Last time, I heard someone was dealing with it but never got a pickup time or a driver's name.
  16. Maya: We will ask for the responsible person and the contact route for the response. If an arrangement changes, we need the actual details, not just an assurance that the request was passed on.
  17. Mr. Ellis: Thank you. I can explain what I was told last week, and you can explain what is on your assignment this morning.
  18. Maya: That will help us report the service discrepancy fairly. I will keep your account separate from what the current record says, so the coordinator can reconcile them.
  19. Mr. Ellis: Please put the outcome in the notes as well. The next person should not have to start the same conversation from the beginning.
  20. Maya: I will update the visit record with the request, contact, and confirmed response. Until we have that response, I will describe transport as unconfirmed rather than booked.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The appointment is not on my assignment

A caregiver arrives for the fictional 9:00 visit and checks an unexpected transport request.

  1. Client: You're here. We need to leave for my clinic appointment in forty minutes.
  2. Caregiver: Your appointment is at ten, correct? I see breakfast and light household tasks on my assignment.
  3. Client: I mentioned the ride when I spoke with someone last week.
  4. Caregiver: You may have discussed a separate arrangement. I don't see transport listed here.
  5. Client: Does that mean nobody is coming to pick me up today?
  6. Caregiver: I haven't confirmed that. May I call the coordinator before we make that assumption?
  7. Client: Yes, please. I don't want to miss another appointment this month.
  8. Caregiver: I'll explain the appointment time and ask what transport, if any, was arranged.
  9. Client: While you check, can I have my breakfast at the table?
  10. Caregiver: Certainly. We'll continue the agreed visit tasks while the coordinator checks the transport details.
A changed start time

A caregiver and coordinator reconcile two different visit times before a planned home visit.

  1. Caregiver: My assigned visit starts at nine tomorrow, but the client's reminder says half past nine.
  2. Coordinator: I changed the visit window yesterday. Your printed schedule may be an older version.
  3. Caregiver: Which time should I confirm with the client before I leave today?
  4. Coordinator: Half past nine. The visit is still two hours, ending at eleven thirty.
  5. Caregiver: The client usually expects breakfast earlier. Has that change been discussed with her?
  6. Coordinator: Not yet. I need to check whether the later time works for her.
  7. Caregiver: Then I shouldn't present nine thirty as an agreed change at this point.
  8. Coordinator: Correct. Please tell her I'm checking the revised arrangement and will call shortly.
  9. Caregiver: I'll distinguish the proposed time from the confirmed plan and wait for your update.
  10. Coordinator: Thank you. I'll confirm the final window with both of you before today's visit ends.
Whose preference is it?

A client explains how they want ordinary household support organized during the visit.

  1. Caregiver: Your plan includes light housekeeping today: the dishes, laundry, and clearing the table. Which would you prefer first?
  2. Client: My daughter always says to start with the laundry, but I'd rather clear the table.
  3. Caregiver: You'd like the table ready before we deal with the laundry. Have I understood?
  4. Client: Exactly. I enjoy sitting there, and the folded towels aren't urgent today.
  5. Caregiver: That order fits today's assignment. Shall we keep your newspapers in their usual basket?
  6. Client: Please leave the top one out. I'm halfway through an article in it.
  7. Caregiver: I'll leave that newspaper on the side table and put the others in the basket.
  8. Client: Thank you for asking. Sometimes things disappear before I've finished using them.
  9. Caregiver: I'll check before moving personal items. Let me know if you'd prefer another arrangement.
  10. Client: That works. Once the table is clear, we can decide which laundry needs attention.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The appointment is real; the transport is unconfirmed
  1. Read Mr. Ellis and Maya aloud, then swap roles. Acknowledge the appointment before explaining the transport limit.
  2. Correct the ten gaps. Read back nine-to-eleven visit, ten-o-clock appointment, and unconfirmed transport as separate facts.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Clarify a different unassigned errand

A 14:00-15:00 visit includes household support. The client expects a parcel pickup, which is not assigned. The coordinator can clarify it; no pickup has been booked.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Caregiver: "This visit ends at ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 15:00

The stated one-hour visit runs from 14:00 to 15:00.

2. Client: "The extra request is a ___."

Answer and explanation

A. parcel pickup

The new expectation concerns a parcel, not an appointment journey.

3. Caregiver: "I will clarify the request with the ___."

Answer and explanation

D. coordinator

The coordinator is identified as the appropriate person to clarify the unassigned request.

4. Caregiver: "The pickup is currently ___."

Answer and explanation

A. unconfirmed

No pickup has been booked, so completion or confirmation would overstate the facts.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Confirming the visit and the care plan
Original fictional case: Caregiver Maya arrives at 9:00 for a two-hour visit with Mr. Ellis. Her assignment lists breakfast support and light household tasks. He expects her to drive him to a 10:00 appointment, but transport is not listed and she has no authorization to provide it. The coordinator is available by phone. Maya must establish the discrepancy, contact the coordinator with the client involved as appropriate, and continue agreed support within her role. Neither a phone message nor a request for help confirms a transport booking.
Language workshop: The appointment is real; the transport is unconfirmed
Communication goal: Clarify scheduled support, acknowledge an unmet expectation, and request authorization without promising an unassigned service.
Language guidance: An appointment time is not a transport booking. A transport request is not authorization for the caregiver to drive. Keep the appointment, departure arrangements, transport provider, and booking status distinct when asking the coordinator to resolve the discrepancy.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Good morning, Mr. Ellis. I am here for your nine-to-eleven visit. / My assignment lists breakfast support and light household tasks. / What were you expecting for your appointment this morning? / I understand that getting there at ten matters to you. / Transport is not listed in the assignment I have. / I do not have authorization to drive you. / I can contact the coordinator now to check the arrangement. / Would you like to be part of that conversation? / There is a difference between the expected support and my recorded assignment. / Has transport actually been booked, or only requested? / Who is confirming the transport arrangement? / Please confirm any access requirements through the agreed process. / I cannot confirm a driver until the coordinator verifies the booking. / While we clarify that, would you like help with breakfast? / Let me check: the appointment is at ten, and transport is still unconfirmed. / I will record the request, who was contacted, and the confirmed response.
Vocabulary: care plan: A record of assessed needs and agreed support arrangements. / assignment: The particular work allocated to a worker. / visit window: The specified period for an expected visit. / scheduled duration: The planned length of a visit or service. / care coordinator: The person coordinating relevant care arrangements. / support need: An area in which a person requires assistance. / ADLs: Activities of daily living, such as dressing or eating. / IADLs: Instrumental activities of daily living, such as shopping or household management. / personal care: Support with personal daily activities within an agreed role. / domestic support: Agreed help with household activities. / light housekeeping: Limited household tasks within the assigned service. / appointment: A planned meeting or service at a specified time. / transport authorization: Permission for the particular transport arrangement. / escort: A person accompanying someone under an agreed arrangement. / scope of duties: The tasks permitted within the worker's actual role. / service discrepancy: A difference between expected and recorded service. / unmet expectation: Something expected that has not been confirmed or provided. / booking confirmation: Evidence that a particular service reservation is accepted. / accessible transport: Transport suited to the person's relevant access needs. / contingency arrangement: A prepared alternative for a possible disruption. / contact route: The approved way to reach the appropriate person. / visit record: The factual account of support and events during a visit. / authorized change: A change agreed through the required process. / read-back: Repeating key information to check that it was understood correctly.
Speaking task: Read Mr. Ellis and Maya aloud, then swap roles. Acknowledge the appointment before explaining the transport limit. Correct the ten gaps. Read back nine-to-eleven visit, ten-o-clock appointment, and unconfirmed transport as separate facts. Repeat the coordinator call and breakfast offer without blaming the client or promising a private lift.
Partner: Mr. Ellis: Client / Maya: Caregiver
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A 14:00-15:00 visit includes household support. The client expects a parcel pickup, which is not assigned. The coordinator can clarify it; no pickup has been booked.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Mr. Ellis: Thank you. I can explain what I was told last week, and you can explain what is on your assignment this morning.
Maya: That will help us report the service discrepancy fairly. I will keep your account separate from what the current record says, so the coordinator can reconcile them.
Mr. Ellis: Please put the outcome in the notes as well. The next person should not have to start the same conversation from the beginning.
Maya: I will update the visit record with the request, contact, and confirmed response. Until we have that response, I will describe transport as unconfirmed rather than booked.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Scope: People, schedules, care plans, and incidents are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not clinical care, medication instructions, capacity assessment, or professional certification. Follow the actual care plan, consent and privacy requirements, training, employer procedures, and qualified clinical advice. For urgent or emergency concerns use the appropriate local service and procedure without waiting for a routine callback. The cited England/UK sources do not establish universal rules.
Lesson 01: Confirming the visit and the care plan
Original fictional case: Caregiver Maya arrives at 9:00 for a two-hour visit with Mr. Ellis. Her assignment lists breakfast support and light household tasks. He expects her to drive him to a 10:00 appointment, but transport is not listed and she has no authorization to provide it. The coordinator is available by phone. Maya must establish the discrepancy, contact the coordinator with the client involved as appropriate, and continue agreed support within her role. Neither a phone message nor a request for help confirms a transport booking.
Language workshop: The appointment is real; the transport is unconfirmed
Communication goal: Clarify scheduled support, acknowledge an unmet expectation, and request authorization without promising an unassigned service.
Language guidance: An appointment time is not a transport booking. A transport request is not authorization for the caregiver to drive. Keep the appointment, departure arrangements, transport provider, and booking status distinct when asking the coordinator to resolve the discrepancy.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Good morning, Mr. Ellis. I am here for your nine-to-eleven visit. / My assignment lists breakfast support and light household tasks. / What were you expecting for your appointment this morning? / I understand that getting there at ten matters to you. / Transport is not listed in the assignment I have. / I do not have authorization to drive you. / I can contact the coordinator now to check the arrangement. / Would you like to be part of that conversation? / There is a difference between the expected support and my recorded assignment. / Has transport actually been booked, or only requested? / Who is confirming the transport arrangement? / Please confirm any access requirements through the agreed process. / I cannot confirm a driver until the coordinator verifies the booking. / While we clarify that, would you like help with breakfast? / Let me check: the appointment is at ten, and transport is still unconfirmed. / I will record the request, who was contacted, and the confirmed response.
Vocabulary: care plan: A record of assessed needs and agreed support arrangements. / assignment: The particular work allocated to a worker. / visit window: The specified period for an expected visit. / scheduled duration: The planned length of a visit or service. / care coordinator: The person coordinating relevant care arrangements. / support need: An area in which a person requires assistance. / ADLs: Activities of daily living, such as dressing or eating. / IADLs: Instrumental activities of daily living, such as shopping or household management. / personal care: Support with personal daily activities within an agreed role. / domestic support: Agreed help with household activities. / light housekeeping: Limited household tasks within the assigned service. / appointment: A planned meeting or service at a specified time. / transport authorization: Permission for the particular transport arrangement. / escort: A person accompanying someone under an agreed arrangement. / scope of duties: The tasks permitted within the worker's actual role. / service discrepancy: A difference between expected and recorded service. / unmet expectation: Something expected that has not been confirmed or provided. / booking confirmation: Evidence that a particular service reservation is accepted. / accessible transport: Transport suited to the person's relevant access needs. / contingency arrangement: A prepared alternative for a possible disruption. / contact route: The approved way to reach the appropriate person. / visit record: The factual account of support and events during a visit. / authorized change: A change agreed through the required process. / read-back: Repeating key information to check that it was understood correctly.
Speaking task: Read Mr. Ellis and Maya aloud, then swap roles. Acknowledge the appointment before explaining the transport limit. Correct the ten gaps. Read back nine-to-eleven visit, ten-o-clock appointment, and unconfirmed transport as separate facts. Repeat the coordinator call and breakfast offer without blaming the client or promising a private lift.
Partner: Mr. Ellis: Client / Maya: Caregiver
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A 14:00-15:00 visit includes household support. The client expects a parcel pickup, which is not assigned. The coordinator can clarify it; no pickup has been booked.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Mr. Ellis: Thank you. I can explain what I was told last week, and you can explain what is on your assignment this morning.
Maya: That will help us report the service discrepancy fairly. I will keep your account separate from what the current record says, so the coordinator can reconcile them.
Mr. Ellis: Please put the outcome in the notes as well. The next person should not have to start the same conversation from the beginning.
Maya: I will update the visit record with the request, contact, and confirmed response. Until we have that response, I will describe transport as unconfirmed rather than booked.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

02Consent, choice, and respectful assistanceTen quiet minutes before choosing clothes+

Your goal

Offer support without pressure, respect a changed preference, and record the agreed timing without judgment.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

Ten quiet minutes before choosing clothes

Caregiver Imani has thirty minutes left in her visit with Ms. Hale. Support with choosing and arranging clothes is included in the plan, but Ms. Hale declines help now and asks for ten quiet minutes before a visitor arrives. There is no immediate safety concern in this scenario. Imani should acknowledge the choice, explain the remaining time, and agree whether to ask again later. A listed task is not permission to override the person. The record should describe what was offered, chosen, and actually provided rather than label the client difficult.

Ms. Hale: Client / Imani: Caregiver

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does Ms. Hale request?

Answer and explanation

A. Ten quiet minutes before help is discussed again

Her stated preference concerns the timing of this task, not the entire care arrangement.

2. How much scheduled visit time remains after ten minutes?

Answer and explanation

A. Twenty minutes

Thirty minutes remaining minus ten quiet minutes leaves twenty scheduled minutes.

3. Which fact is explicitly supplied?

Answer and explanation

C. There is no immediate safety concern in this scenario.

The brief excludes an immediate safety concern but does not establish incapacity or substitute consent.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Declining one offer now does not automatically cancel every later offer or the whole care plan. Ask about the specific task and timing. Respect the actual response; a convenient schedule does not turn refusal into consent.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
consent
Agreement to a particular action through the applicable process.seek consent
withdraw consent
Change an earlier agreement so that the action is no longer agreed.respect withdrawn consent
decline assistance
Say that offered help is not wanted.acknowledge declined assistance
preference
A person's stated choice or favored way of doing something.respect a preference
autonomy
A person's ability and right to direct relevant choices about their life.support autonomy
dignity
Respect for a person's worth, privacy, and personhood.preserve dignity
person-centered support
Support organized around the person's needs, choices, and circumstances.provide person-centered support
supported choice
A decision made with appropriate communication or practical assistance.enable supported choice
decision-specific
Relating to the particular decision, not every possible choice.use decision-specific language
capacity
Ability to make a particular decision under the applicable legal framework.refer a capacity concern
coercion
Pressure or force that undermines free choice.avoid coercion
assent
An expression of agreement whose legal significance depends on context.distinguish assent from legal consent
verbal agreement
Agreement expressed in spoken words.confirm verbal agreement
nonverbal response
Communication through gesture, expression, or other nonspoken behavior.notice a nonverbal response
communication need
A requirement that helps a person understand or express themselves.meet a communication need
processing time
Time needed to understand information and formulate a response.allow processing time
privacy preference
A stated choice about personal space or access to information.clarify a privacy preference
reoffer
Offer support again without assuming the answer will change.agree when to reoffer
time remaining
The portion of the scheduled visit not yet used.explain the time remaining
partial support
Help with only the agreed part of a task.offer partial support
independence
Doing or directing activities with the appropriate degree of support.promote independence
judgmental label
A description that criticizes a person rather than reporting events.avoid judgmental labels
neutral wording
Language that describes facts without an unsupported evaluation.use neutral wording
agreed pause
A mutually understood break before an activity is discussed or resumed.respect an agreed pause

Do not infer lack of decision-making capacity from disagreement, age, accent, or a choice you dislike. Any genuine concern belongs in the applicable assessment and escalation process, not an improvised judgment used to make the task easier.

C Notice the language

Respect does not depend on agreement

A worker may feel pressure to complete every listed task. Use an adult-to-adult tone and separate the service schedule from the person's choice. A preference that differs from yours is not evidence that the person cannot decide.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Offer help
Would you like help choosing your clothes?
Accept the answer
That is fine; I will not start now.
Check the preference
Would you prefer a few quiet minutes first?
Explain the schedule
There are thirty minutes left in this visit.
Make timing clear
If we pause for ten minutes, twenty minutes will remain.
Ask about a later offer
Would you like me to ask again after that?
Avoid pressure
You do not need to agree just to complete my task list.
Clarify a smaller task
Would help laying out one item be useful, or would you prefer no help?
Respect privacy
Where would you like me to wait during the pause?
Check a changed choice
Is that still what you would like?
Address the person directly
I would like to hear your preference first.
Avoid a label
I will record the help offered and your response.
Distinguish the scope
You are declining this help now, not necessarily every later offer.
Acknowledge independence
You can choose what you want to wear.
Keep a boundary honest
I cannot promise extra visit time without an authorized change.
Confirm the agreement
We will pause for ten minutes, and I will ask again as agreed.
6 language and precision notes
Would you like
Makes the offer a genuine choice rather than a disguised instruction.
I will not start
Confirms that the worker heard and will respect the current response.
If we pause
Explains the time consequence without using it as a threat.
Ask again
Requests permission to reoffer rather than announcing repeated pressure.
Your preference first
Keeps the person central even when a visitor or relative is present.
Help offered and your response
Supports factual recording instead of a judgment about personality.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which record is most accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. Clothes support offered; Ms. Hale requested a ten-minute pause and agreed to a later offer.

The factual record preserves the specific offer, response, and timing without a judgmental or exaggerated conclusion.

2. Which reply avoids pressure?

Answer and explanation

D. I will pause; would you like me to ask again in ten minutes?

Permission to ask again is not permission to begin later, continue during the pause, or use a relative to pressure the person.

3. What does refusal alone establish here?

Answer and explanation

C. The offered help is not wanted at this moment.

The refusal concerns this offer and time, not a diagnosis or global decision-making ability.

4. Which timing statement is correct?

Answer and explanation

D. Twenty scheduled minutes remain after the agreed ten-minute pause.

The pause uses ten of the thirty remaining minutes and does not automatically extend the visit.

Check explanations in the book, page 87 ↗

E Conversations

Ten quiet minutes before choosing clothes

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ms. Hale: Client / Imani: Caregiver

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • privacy preference
  • preference
  • partial support
  • neutral wording
  • reoffer
  • agreed pause
  • independence
  • withdraw consent
  • verbal agreement
  • processing time
  1. Ms. Hale
    Please leave the clothes for now, Imani. My visitor is coming later, but I would like ten quiet minutes before we do anything else.
  2. Imani
    Of course. I will respect that 1 and will not start arranging them. Would you like me to ask again after ten minutes, or would you prefer not to discuss clothes today?
  3. Ms. Hale
    Ask me again after the pause. I might just need help finding the blue cardigan, but I want to sit quietly first.
  4. Imani
    We can agree on that 2. There are thirty minutes left in my visit, so twenty will remain afterward. I want you to know the timing without feeling rushed.
  5. Ms. Hale
    I know you have a list. But when I say not now, will the notes say I was being difficult? That worries me.
  6. Imani
    I can use 3 in the record. I will describe what I offered and what you requested, rather than give you a negative label for making a choice.
  7. Ms. Hale
    That would help. I still want to choose my own clothes. Having someone here should not mean everything gets decided for me.
  8. Imani
    Your 4 matters. I can help with an agreed part of the task, such as finding the cardigan, without taking over decisions you want to make yourself.
  9. Ms. Hale
    Could you wait in the sitting room? I would rather not have someone sorting things behind me while I am trying to relax.
  10. Imani
    Yes, that is a clear 5. I will not use the pause to start the task anyway. Let me know if you want to change that arrangement.
  11. Ms. Hale
    My niece sometimes says I should accept whatever help is offered. She means well, but I want you to ask me directly.
  12. Imani
    I will. Your answer to this offer is the relevant 6 we are discussing. I will not treat another person's opinion as permission to ignore what you have just said.
  13. Ms. Hale
    And if I agree later but then want you to stop, can I say so? Some days I change my mind halfway through.
  14. Imani
    Yes. You can 7. Tell me if you no longer want the help, and I will respond to that rather than assume an earlier yes lasts regardless of what you say.
  15. Ms. Hale
    Thank you. A quiet ten minutes is all I am asking for. I do not want to spend it explaining why I need it.
  16. Imani
    I will allow that 8. A quiet pause can be part of respectful communication. There is no need to repeat the same question every minute while you are deciding.
  17. Ms. Hale
    After the ten minutes, I think finding the cardigan will be enough. I can decide about the rest when you ask me.
  18. Imani
    That would be 9. We can confirm it then, and I will be honest about the remaining visit time rather than promise an extension that has not been authorized.
  19. Ms. Hale
    Good. Let us take the quiet time now, and then you can check with me again before touching the clothes.
  20. Imani
    We have an 10 of ten minutes. I will wait in the sitting room, ask again afterward, and record the support you actually choose and receive.

Answers and explanations
  1. preference Turn 2. The preference concerns a quiet pause before clothing support, not a refusal of every service.
  2. reoffer Turn 4. A reoffer is a later invitation to accept help, not an assumption that the person will agree.
  3. neutral wording Turn 6. Neutral wording reports the offer and response without turning a personal choice into criticism.
  4. independence Turn 8. Independence includes directing the support and choosing clothes, not necessarily completing every movement without help.
  5. privacy preference Turn 10. The privacy preference specifies where the worker should wait and excludes continuing the task during the pause.
  6. verbal agreement Turn 12. Verbal agreement expresses the person's response to the specific offer; a relative's general opinion is not a substitute.
  7. withdraw consent Turn 14. Withdrawing consent changes the earlier agreement; the worker must follow the actual consent process rather than rely on an old yes.
  8. processing time Turn 16. Processing time allows the person space to respond and choose without repeated prompts or pressure.
  9. partial support Turn 18. Partial support covers only the agreed part of the task, such as finding one garment.
  10. agreed pause Turn 20. The agreed pause is ten minutes with permission to ask again afterward, not permission to begin the task automatically.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ms. Hale: Please leave the clothes for now, Imani. My visitor is coming later, but I would like ten quiet minutes before we do anything else.
  2. Imani: Of course. I will respect that preference and will not start arranging them. Would you like me to ask again after ten minutes, or would you prefer not to discuss clothes today?
  3. Ms. Hale: Ask me again after the pause. I might just need help finding the blue cardigan, but I want to sit quietly first.
  4. Imani: We can agree on that reoffer. There are thirty minutes left in my visit, so twenty will remain afterward. I want you to know the timing without feeling rushed.
  5. Ms. Hale: I know you have a list. But when I say not now, will the notes say I was being difficult? That worries me.
  6. Imani: I can use neutral wording in the record. I will describe what I offered and what you requested, rather than give you a negative label for making a choice.
  7. Ms. Hale: That would help. I still want to choose my own clothes. Having someone here should not mean everything gets decided for me.
  8. Imani: Your independence matters. I can help with an agreed part of the task, such as finding the cardigan, without taking over decisions you want to make yourself.
  9. Ms. Hale: Could you wait in the sitting room? I would rather not have someone sorting things behind me while I am trying to relax.
  10. Imani: Yes, that is a clear privacy preference. I will not use the pause to start the task anyway. Let me know if you want to change that arrangement.
  11. Ms. Hale: My niece sometimes says I should accept whatever help is offered. She means well, but I want you to ask me directly.
  12. Imani: I will. Your answer to this offer is the relevant verbal agreement we are discussing. I will not treat another person's opinion as permission to ignore what you have just said.
  13. Ms. Hale: And if I agree later but then want you to stop, can I say so? Some days I change my mind halfway through.
  14. Imani: Yes. You can withdraw consent. Tell me if you no longer want the help, and I will respond to that rather than assume an earlier yes lasts regardless of what you say.
  15. Ms. Hale: Thank you. A quiet ten minutes is all I am asking for. I do not want to spend it explaining why I need it.
  16. Imani: I will allow that processing time. A quiet pause can be part of respectful communication. There is no need to repeat the same question every minute while you are deciding.
  17. Ms. Hale: After the ten minutes, I think finding the cardigan will be enough. I can decide about the rest when you ask me.
  18. Imani: That would be partial support. We can confirm it then, and I will be honest about the remaining visit time rather than promise an extension that has not been authorized.
  19. Ms. Hale: Good. Let us take the quiet time now, and then you can check with me again before touching the clothes.
  20. Imani: We have an agreed pause of ten minutes. I will wait in the sitting room, ask again afterward, and record the support you actually choose and receive.
Dialogue in the book, page 19 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Ten minutes before we start

A client requests quiet time during a scheduled visit with thirty minutes remaining.

  1. Client: I'd rather not sort my clothes right now. I've had people coming in all morning.
  2. Caregiver: You'd like a break from activity. Would ten quiet minutes help before we discuss it again?
  3. Client: Yes, but I don't want you standing beside the chair while I rest.
  4. Caregiver: Of course. I can wait in the kitchen if that suits you and the visit arrangement.
  5. Client: That's fine. Please don't start opening drawers while I'm having my break.
  6. Caregiver: I won't. Your personal space matters. We have thirty minutes left, so we can choose a smaller task afterward.
  7. Client: And if I decide not to do the clothes at all today?
  8. Caregiver: I'll respect that choice. We can confirm what other agreed support you want instead.
  9. Client: Good. Please come back in ten minutes and ask me then.
  10. Caregiver: Ten minutes in the kitchen, then I'll check with you before we do anything else.
Let me finish the sentence

A caregiver repairs an overly hurried offer of help with a household task.

  1. Caregiver: Shall I put those books away for you before your visitor arrives this afternoon?
  2. Client: I was going to say that I want the green one left out.
  3. Caregiver: I'm sorry; I started moving them before you finished. I'll stop and listen.
  4. Client: The green book stays here, and the other two belong on the lower shelf.
  5. Caregiver: Green book here, two books on the lower shelf. Is that the arrangement you want?
  6. Client: Yes. I know it seems small, but I like to find things myself.
  7. Caregiver: That makes sense. Would you like me to move those two now, or leave them?
  8. Client: You can move them now. Just keep them where I can see their covers.
  9. Caregiver: I'll place them with their covers visible and ask you to check the position.
  10. Client: Thank you. That's the help I wanted, without everything being reorganized around me.
The family member answers first

A caregiver makes room for the client's own preference during a relative's visit.

  1. Relative: She wants you to rearrange the whole cupboard today. We discussed it yesterday evening.
  2. Caregiver: Thank you. I'd like to check her preference directly before we agree on that task.
  3. Relative: She's tired, so I'm trying to save her from having to explain everything.
  4. Caregiver: I appreciate that. We can keep the question short and give her time to answer.
  5. Relative: What if she changes her mind after we have already started moving things?
  6. Caregiver: Then we'll pause and ask what she wants to keep or change before continuing.
  7. Relative: All right. She may only want the cups moved onto the lower shelf.
  8. Caregiver: That would be a more specific request. I'll ask her which items she wants within reach.
  9. Relative: I'll let her answer, and I can explain yesterday's discussion if she asks me.
  10. Caregiver: Thank you. We can support the conversation without replacing her own choice with our assumptions.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Ten quiet minutes before choosing clothes
  1. Read Ms. Hale and Imani aloud, then swap roles. Make the quiet pause sound like an accepted choice, not a negotiation to overcome.
  2. Correct the ten gaps. State thirty minutes left, ten minutes requested, and twenty remaining without implying an automatic extension.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Respond to a request for a shorter pause

There are twenty-five minutes left. The client requests five quiet minutes and agrees to a later offer of help with a coat. No immediate safety concern is supplied.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Caregiver: "We will pause for ___ minutes."

Answer and explanation

B. five

The client requested a five-minute pause before another offer.

2. Client: "Please ask again about help with my ___."

Answer and explanation

B. coat

The specified later offer concerns a coat, not all clothing tasks.

3. Caregiver: "That leaves ___ scheduled minutes."

Answer and explanation

B. twenty

Twenty-five minutes remaining minus a five-minute pause leaves twenty.

4. Caregiver: "I will record the offer and response using ___."

Answer and explanation

D. neutral wording

Factual language records the specific choice without criticizing the person.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Home Care & Caregiving English
Professional audience: Home care aides, personal care assistants, and caregivers
Scope: People, schedules, care plans, and incidents are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not clinical care, medication instructions, capacity assessment, or professional certification. Follow the actual care plan, consent and privacy requirements, training, employer procedures, and qualified clinical advice. For urgent or emergency concerns use the appropriate local service and procedure without waiting for a routine callback. The cited England/UK sources do not establish universal rules.
Lesson 02: Consent, choice, and respectful assistance
Original fictional case: Caregiver Imani has thirty minutes left in her visit with Ms. Hale. Support with choosing and arranging clothes is included in the plan, but Ms. Hale declines help now and asks for ten quiet minutes before a visitor arrives. There is no immediate safety concern in this scenario. Imani should acknowledge the choice, explain the remaining time, and agree whether to ask again later. A listed task is not permission to override the person. The record should describe what was offered, chosen, and actually provided rather than label the client difficult.
Language workshop: Ten quiet minutes before choosing clothes
Communication goal: Offer support without pressure, respect a changed preference, and record the agreed timing without judgment.
Language guidance: Declining one offer now does not automatically cancel every later offer or the whole care plan. Ask about the specific task and timing. Respect the actual response; a convenient schedule does not turn refusal into consent.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Would you like help choosing your clothes? / That is fine; I will not start now. / Would you prefer a few quiet minutes first? / There are thirty minutes left in this visit. / If we pause for ten minutes, twenty minutes will remain. / Would you like me to ask again after that? / You do not need to agree just to complete my task list. / Would help laying out one item be useful, or would you prefer no help? / Where would you like me to wait during the pause? / Is that still what you would like? / I would like to hear your preference first. / I will record the help offered and your response. / You are declining this help now, not necessarily every later offer. / You can choose what you want to wear. / I cannot promise extra visit time without an authorized change. / We will pause for ten minutes, and I will ask again as agreed.
Vocabulary: consent: Agreement to a particular action through the applicable process. / withdraw consent: Change an earlier agreement so that the action is no longer agreed. / decline assistance: Say that offered help is not wanted. / preference: A person's stated choice or favored way of doing something. / autonomy: A person's ability and right to direct relevant choices about their life. / dignity: Respect for a person's worth, privacy, and personhood. / person-centered support: Support organized around the person's needs, choices, and circumstances. / supported choice: A decision made with appropriate communication or practical assistance. / decision-specific: Relating to the particular decision, not every possible choice. / capacity: Ability to make a particular decision under the applicable legal framework. / coercion: Pressure or force that undermines free choice. / assent: An expression of agreement whose legal significance depends on context. / verbal agreement: Agreement expressed in spoken words. / nonverbal response: Communication through gesture, expression, or other nonspoken behavior. / communication need: A requirement that helps a person understand or express themselves. / processing time: Time needed to understand information and formulate a response. / privacy preference: A stated choice about personal space or access to information. / reoffer: Offer support again without assuming the answer will change. / time remaining: The portion of the scheduled visit not yet used. / partial support: Help with only the agreed part of a task. / independence: Doing or directing activities with the appropriate degree of support. / judgmental label: A description that criticizes a person rather than reporting events. / neutral wording: Language that describes facts without an unsupported evaluation. / agreed pause: A mutually understood break before an activity is discussed or resumed.
Speaking task: Read Ms. Hale and Imani aloud, then swap roles. Make the quiet pause sound like an accepted choice, not a negotiation to overcome. Correct the ten gaps. State thirty minutes left, ten minutes requested, and twenty remaining without implying an automatic extension. Repeat the later offer of cardigan support, asking again before touching the clothes and keeping the record neutral.
Partner: Ms. Hale: Client / Imani: Caregiver
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: There are twenty-five minutes left. The client requests five quiet minutes and agrees to a later offer of help with a coat. No immediate safety concern is supplied.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ms. Hale: After the ten minutes, I think finding the cardigan will be enough. I can decide about the rest when you ask me.
Imani: That would be partial support. We can confirm it then, and I will be honest about the remaining visit time rather than promise an extension that has not been authorized.
Ms. Hale: Good. Let us take the quiet time now, and then you can check with me again before touching the clothes.
Imani: We have an agreed pause of ten minutes. I will wait in the sitting room, ask again afterward, and record the support you actually choose and receive.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
