03Reporting a change without diagnosingA precise report without an invented cause+

Your goal

Attribute the client's words, describe a change from usual behavior, and seek the appropriate response without diagnosing.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

A precise report without an invented cause

During a routine visit, caregiver Luis hears Ms. Chen say, "I feel more tired than yesterday." At 9:20, he notices that she stops twice while describing breakfast, which is unusual during his visits. He contacts nurse Jo through the local reporting procedure. He has no clinical diagnosis and has not measured vital signs. No emergency signs are specified in this fictional brief; that is not proof that the person is safe. Any urgent or emergency concern must follow the local urgent route without waiting for routine reporting.

Luis: Caregiver / Jo: Nurse receiving the report

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which statement comes directly from the client?

Answer and explanation

A. I feel more tired than yesterday.

The brief supplies a direct report of tiredness, not a diagnosis or measured assessment.

2. What did Luis observe?

Answer and explanation

B. Two pauses while Ms. Chen described breakfast at 9:20

The observed event is the two pauses, with a stated time and context.

3. What must happen if an urgent concern arises?

Answer and explanation

B. Use the appropriate local urgent or emergency route without waiting for routine reporting.

The routine reporting exercise does not replace timely local action for urgent or emergency concerns.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Not measured is not the same as normal. No emergency signs specified in a teaching brief is not a real clinical clearance. Report the actual known information and follow the local urgent pathway whenever the circumstances require it.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
observation
Something the worker directly notices, distinguished from an interpretation.report an observation
reported symptom
An experience described by the person, not necessarily measured by the listener.attribute a reported symptom
direct quotation
The person's actual words presented as their statement.preserve a direct quotation
onset
When a change or symptom began, if known.clarify the reported onset
baseline
The usual pattern used for a relevant comparison.describe the known baseline
change from usual
A difference from the person's previously observed pattern.report a change from usual
frequency
How often something occurs in the stated period.record the observed frequency
duration
How long an event lasts, when this is known.clarify the duration
context
The circumstances in which an event happened.provide relevant context
objective description
A specific factual account rather than an unsupported conclusion.use an objective description
subjective report
An experience described from the person's perspective.attribute a subjective report
clinical assessment
Evaluation by an appropriately qualified professional.request clinical assessment
diagnosis
Identification of a condition through an appropriate clinical process.avoid an unsupported diagnosis
vital signs
Clinical measurements such as pulse or temperature, taken within an authorized role.report measured vital signs
unmeasured
Not measured; different from a normal result.identify unmeasured information
uncertainty
Something not yet established or known.state uncertainty
red flag
A sign or concern requiring particular attention under the relevant procedure.recognize a red flag
escalation
Raising a concern through the appropriate route for action.make a timely escalation
urgent pathway
The designated process for concerns requiring prompt action.use the urgent pathway
callback
A return call that may still be pending.request a callback
SBAR
Situation, background, assessment, recommendation; a structured communication framework used in some services.use the local SBAR format
read-back confirmation
Repetition of key information followed by a check of accuracy.request read-back confirmation
contemporaneous note
A record made at or near the time of the event.make a contemporaneous note
follow-up instruction
Direction about the next action from the appropriate person.clarify follow-up instructions

Separate what the person said, what you saw, and what you do not know. If your service uses SBAR, keep the assessment portion within your role: describe the concern and observation rather than invent a diagnosis or a measurement.

C Notice the language

Specific observations travel better than labels

Words such as fine, poorly, confused, or not herself may hide the information the next person needs. Give the person's own words, the observable event, the time, and the comparison you can genuinely support. Let qualified staff assess the cause.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Open the report
I am calling about a change I noticed during this visit.
Identify the source
Ms. Chen told me she feels more tired than yesterday.
Quote accurately
Her exact words were, I feel more tired than yesterday.
Describe the observation
She stopped twice while describing breakfast.
Give the time
I noticed this at nine twenty.
Bound the comparison
That is unusual during my visits with her.
State a limit
I do not know what is causing it.
Avoid a false measurement
I have not measured her vital signs.
Clarify onset
I know when I noticed it, but not when it began.
Request the next step
What should happen next under the care plan and reporting procedure?
Check receipt
Can you confirm that you have received the concern?
Read back an instruction
Let me repeat the instruction to check I understood it.
Correct an inference
I reported tiredness; I did not diagnose the cause.
Keep urgency separate
I will use the urgent route if the concern requires it.
Document the exchange
I will record the observation, time, contact, and instructions received.
Avoid false closure
The report is received; that does not mean a clinical assessment is complete.
6 language and precision notes
Told me
Attributes an experience to the person rather than presenting it as a measured finding.
While describing breakfast
Supplies context for the observation without inventing its cause.
During my visits
Bounds the baseline to the worker's actual experience.
Have not measured
Keeps missing data distinct from reassuring results.
When I noticed it
Distinguishes observation time from symptom onset.
Received versus assessed
Prevents administrative receipt from being mistaken for clinical evaluation.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which report is most precise?

Answer and explanation

A. At 9:20 she paused twice while describing breakfast and said she felt more tired than yesterday.

The precise account combines time, observable behavior, and attributed words without inventing a cause.

2. How should missing vital signs be described?

Answer and explanation

A. Not measured by Luis

The absence of measurements cannot be converted into normal findings or clinical clearance.

3. Which time is established?

Answer and explanation

A. When Luis noticed the pauses

The brief gives an observation time, while the onset and assessment remain unspecified.

4. What does receipt of the report establish?

Answer and explanation

D. The concern reached the receiving person, not that assessment is complete.

Communication receipt and clinical evaluation are separate stages with different meanings.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

A precise report without an invented cause

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Luis: Caregiver / Jo: Nurse receiving the report

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • read-back confirmation
  • diagnosis
  • objective description
  • onset
  • unmeasured
  • baseline
  • clinical assessment
  • reported symptom
  • contemporaneous note
  • urgent pathway
  1. Luis
    Jo, I am calling through the reporting line about Ms. Chen. I noticed a change during this morning's visit and want to pass on the exact information.
  2. Jo
    Start with the 1 in her own words, then tell me what you directly observed. Keeping those two sources separate will make the report easier to assess.
  3. Luis
    She said, I feel more tired than yesterday. While she was describing breakfast, she stopped twice. That was at nine twenty this morning.
  4. Jo
    Thank you. That gives an 2 of the pauses and an attributed account of how she feels. Do you know when the tiredness actually began?
  5. Luis
    No. I know when we had the conversation, but I cannot say when she first started feeling tired or whether it changed overnight.
  6. Jo
    Then the 3 is not established. Keep nine twenty as the observation time, rather than enter it as the start of the symptom.
  7. Luis
    She does not usually pause like that when we talk during my visits. I have not watched her continuously outside those visits, though.
  8. Jo
    That is a useful, bounded 4. Describe what is unusual in your experience without implying that you know her behavior throughout the whole day or night.
  9. Luis
    I nearly wrote coming down with something. Let me correct that: she reported tiredness and I noticed the pauses. I do not know the cause.
  10. Jo
    Please do not substitute a 5 for the observations. The report should preserve the tiredness, pauses, timing, and uncertainty so the appropriate professional can assess the concern.
  11. Luis
    I also have no temperature, pulse, or other measurements to report. Taking those measurements is not part of what I have done this morning.
  12. Jo
    Record those as 6, not normal. A blank measurement field cannot support reassurance, and you should not invent a reading to make the record look complete.
  13. Luis
    I understand. If the situation becomes urgent while I am trying to reach someone, I should not wait just because a routine message is already in the system.
  14. Jo
    Correct. Use the local 7 when required, including emergency services where appropriate. Routine reporting and urgent response serve different needs; an earlier message does not remove that distinction.
  15. Luis
    Can you confirm that you have received this report? I also need the next instructions through our care procedure so I know what to record.
  16. Jo
    I have received it. We must distinguish that from a completed 8. I will address the next steps through our clinical process; do not describe this call as an assessment outcome.
  17. Luis
    Please give me the next steps one at a time. I will repeat them back; if I miss a word, I want to check it now.
  18. Jo
    That 9 is helpful. Ask immediately if a direction, responsibility, or timing is unclear, and keep any action within the responsibilities you are authorized to perform.
  19. Luis
    My record will include her words, the two pauses, nine twenty, what I do not know, and this contact. It will not say that a cause has been confirmed.
  20. Jo
    Good. Make a 10 and record the actual instructions and response, not an expected outcome. Continue to use the appropriate local route if the concern changes.

Answers and explanations
  1. reported symptom Turn 2. The reported symptom is Ms. Chen's description of tiredness, which should remain attributed to her.
  2. objective description Turn 4. The objective description states the two pauses, their context, and the observation time without assigning a cause.
  3. onset Turn 6. Onset means when the change began; the time the worker noticed it does not automatically establish that.
  4. baseline Turn 8. The baseline is Luis's usual observation during visits, not a claim about every moment outside them.
  5. diagnosis Turn 10. A diagnosis is a clinical conclusion; Luis's observations do not establish a specific cause of the change.
  6. unmeasured Turn 12. Unmeasured describes missing measurements and must not be translated into normal or reassuring results.
  7. urgent pathway Turn 14. The urgent pathway addresses concerns requiring prompt action and is not replaced by a pending routine message.
  8. clinical assessment Turn 16. Clinical assessment is the relevant professional evaluation, not merely acknowledgment that the report arrived.
  9. read-back confirmation Turn 18. Read-back confirmation checks whether the instructions were understood accurately before they are recorded or acted upon.
  10. contemporaneous note Turn 20. A contemporaneous note preserves the actual observation and communication near the event, including limits and instructions received.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Luis: Jo, I am calling through the reporting line about Ms. Chen. I noticed a change during this morning's visit and want to pass on the exact information.
  2. Jo: Start with the reported symptom in her own words, then tell me what you directly observed. Keeping those two sources separate will make the report easier to assess.
  3. Luis: She said, I feel more tired than yesterday. While she was describing breakfast, she stopped twice. That was at nine twenty this morning.
  4. Jo: Thank you. That gives an objective description of the pauses and an attributed account of how she feels. Do you know when the tiredness actually began?
  5. Luis: No. I know when we had the conversation, but I cannot say when she first started feeling tired or whether it changed overnight.
  6. Jo: Then the onset is not established. Keep nine twenty as the observation time, rather than enter it as the start of the symptom.
  7. Luis: She does not usually pause like that when we talk during my visits. I have not watched her continuously outside those visits, though.
  8. Jo: That is a useful, bounded baseline. Describe what is unusual in your experience without implying that you know her behavior throughout the whole day or night.
  9. Luis: I nearly wrote coming down with something. Let me correct that: she reported tiredness and I noticed the pauses. I do not know the cause.
  10. Jo: Please do not substitute a diagnosis for the observations. The report should preserve the tiredness, pauses, timing, and uncertainty so the appropriate professional can assess the concern.
  11. Luis: I also have no temperature, pulse, or other measurements to report. Taking those measurements is not part of what I have done this morning.
  12. Jo: Record those as unmeasured, not normal. A blank measurement field cannot support reassurance, and you should not invent a reading to make the record look complete.
  13. Luis: I understand. If the situation becomes urgent while I am trying to reach someone, I should not wait just because a routine message is already in the system.
  14. Jo: Correct. Use the local urgent pathway when required, including emergency services where appropriate. Routine reporting and urgent response serve different needs; an earlier message does not remove that distinction.
  15. Luis: Can you confirm that you have received this report? I also need the next instructions through our care procedure so I know what to record.
  16. Jo: I have received it. We must distinguish that from a completed clinical assessment. I will address the next steps through our clinical process; do not describe this call as an assessment outcome.
  17. Luis: Please give me the next steps one at a time. I will repeat them back; if I miss a word, I want to check it now.
  18. Jo: That read-back confirmation is helpful. Ask immediately if a direction, responsibility, or timing is unclear, and keep any action within the responsibilities you are authorized to perform.
  19. Luis: My record will include her words, the two pauses, nine twenty, what I do not know, and this contact. It will not say that a cause has been confirmed.
  20. Jo: Good. Make a contemporaneous note and record the actual instructions and response, not an expected outcome. Continue to use the appropriate local route if the concern changes.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

What did you actually notice?

A caregiver gives a factual phone report to a supervisor using the fictional case details.

  1. Supervisor: Your message says the client seems different today. What exactly have you noticed during the visit?
  2. Caregiver: She said, 'I feel more tired than yesterday,' and paused twice while describing breakfast to me.
  3. Supervisor: How does that compare with her usual baseline during your morning visits? Are those pauses typical?
  4. Caregiver: Not during my usual visits, but I don't know whether someone else has noticed them before.
  5. Supervisor: When did this conversation happen, and have you recorded the client's own words accurately?
  6. Caregiver: It happened at nine fifteen. Those are her words, and I haven't added a possible diagnosis.
  7. Supervisor: Thank you. We need the authorized clinician's direction through our agreed reporting process.
  8. Caregiver: Understood. I can provide the time and observations, but I cannot determine what caused the change.
  9. Supervisor: Stay within the local procedure, and use the urgent escalation route if an urgent concern arises.
  10. Caregiver: I'll follow that procedure and confirm who receives this report rather than assuming the message is enough.
A quieter morning

A caregiver checks the meaning of a client's unusual silence without attributing a cause.

  1. Caregiver: You've been quieter than during my recent visits. Would you like some quiet, or is something different today?
  2. Client: I'm thinking about a family matter. I don't feel like having much conversation this morning.
  3. Caregiver: Thank you for telling me. Would you prefer that I keep questions limited to the support you need?
  4. Client: Yes, please. I still want breakfast at the table, just without the usual chat today.
  5. Caregiver: We can do that. Let me know if there is any change or anything you want me to report.
  6. Client: I will. Please don't tell the coordinator that I'm upset with you, because I'm not.
  7. Caregiver: I won't assume that. I'll describe your stated preference rather than give a reason you haven't given.
  8. Client: That's helpful. People sometimes decide what my silence means before they ask me about it.
  9. Caregiver: I'll check instead of guessing. Is there anything else about today's visit you'd like adjusted?
  10. Client: No, the usual support is fine. A quieter visit is all I'm asking for right now.
A secondhand description

A coordinator helps a caregiver distinguish a relative's comment from a direct observation.

  1. Caregiver: The client's brother said she seemed unsteady yesterday, but I wasn't present during his visit.
  2. Coordinator: Then identify him as the source. Have you noticed any change yourself during today's visit?
  3. Caregiver: I haven't observed the situation he described. That doesn't establish that it didn't happen yesterday.
  4. Coordinator: Exactly. What words did he use, and did he say when he noticed it?
  5. Caregiver: He said, 'She seemed unsteady after lunch.' He didn't give a more specific time.
  6. Coordinator: Please keep that distinction clear when you pass the concern through the agreed reporting route.
  7. Caregiver: I'll say it is his report, that the timing is approximate, and that I did not witness it.
  8. Coordinator: Good. Don't label the earlier report as false simply because your own observation differs today.
  9. Caregiver: Understood. I'll avoid combining the two accounts into a single conclusion about her condition.
  10. Coordinator: That will help the authorized person assess what information is available and what still needs clarification.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A precise report without an invented cause
  1. Read Luis and Jo aloud, then swap roles. Keep the client's tiredness report separate from the two observed pauses.
  2. Correct the ten gaps. Distinguish nine twenty as observation time from an unknown onset and unmeasured signs from normal results.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Keep a second observation separate from its cause

At 14:10, a client says, "My usual shoes feel tighter today." The caregiver notices the client trying twice to fasten them. No cause or measurements are established; the local reporting route applies.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Caregiver: "The observation time is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 14:10

The supplied time concerns when the caregiver noticed the event.

2. Receiver: "The client reported that the shoes felt ___."

Answer and explanation

D. tighter today

These words preserve the person's stated experience rather than invent a clinical finding.

3. Caregiver: "I observed ___ attempts to fasten them."

Answer and explanation

B. two

Two fastening attempts are the specified direct observation.

4. Receiver: "The cause remains ___."

Answer and explanation

B. unconfirmed

The scenario supplies no assessment establishing the cause of the reported change.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Course: Home Care & Caregiving English
Professional audience: Home care aides, personal care assistants, and caregivers
Scope: People, schedules, care plans, and incidents are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not clinical care, medication instructions, capacity assessment, or professional certification. Lesson 03: Reporting a change without diagnosing
Original fictional case: During a routine visit, caregiver Luis hears Ms. Chen say, "I feel more tired than yesterday." At 9:20, he notices that she stops twice while describing breakfast, which is unusual during his visits. He contacts nurse Jo through the local reporting procedure. He has no clinical diagnosis and has not measured vital signs. No emergency signs are specified in this fictional brief; that is not proof that the person is safe. Any urgent or emergency concern must follow the local urgent route without waiting for routine reporting.
Language workshop: A precise report without an invented cause
Communication goal: Attribute the client's words, describe a change from usual behavior, and seek the appropriate response without diagnosing.
Language guidance: Not measured is not the same as normal. No emergency signs specified in a teaching brief is not a real clinical clearance. Report the actual known information and follow the local urgent pathway whenever the circumstances require it.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I am calling about a change I noticed during this visit. / Ms. Chen told me she feels more tired than yesterday. / Her exact words were, I feel more tired than yesterday. / She stopped twice while describing breakfast. / I noticed this at nine twenty. / That is unusual during my visits with her. / I do not know what is causing it. / I have not measured her vital signs. / I know when I noticed it, but not when it began. / What should happen next under the care plan and reporting procedure? / Can you confirm that you have received the concern? / Let me repeat the instruction to check I understood it. / I reported tiredness; I did not diagnose the cause. / I will use the urgent route if the concern requires it. / I will record the observation, time, contact, and instructions received. / The report is received; that does not mean a clinical assessment is complete.
Vocabulary: observation: Something the worker directly notices, distinguished from an interpretation. / reported symptom: An experience described by the person, not necessarily measured by the listener. / direct quotation: The person's actual words presented as their statement. / onset: When a change or symptom began, if known. / baseline: The usual pattern used for a relevant comparison. / change from usual: A difference from the person's previously observed pattern. / frequency: How often something occurs in the stated period. / duration: How long an event lasts, when this is known. / context: The circumstances in which an event happened. / objective description: A specific factual account rather than an unsupported conclusion. / subjective report: An experience described from the person's perspective. / clinical assessment: Evaluation by an appropriately qualified professional. / diagnosis: Identification of a condition through an appropriate clinical process. / vital signs: Clinical measurements such as pulse or temperature, taken within an authorized role. / unmeasured: Not measured; different from a normal result. / uncertainty: Something not yet established or known. / red flag: A sign or concern requiring particular attention under the relevant procedure. / escalation: Raising a concern through the appropriate route for action. / urgent pathway: The designated process for concerns requiring prompt action. / callback: A return call that may still be pending. / SBAR: Situation, background, assessment, recommendation; a structured communication framework used in some services. / read-back confirmation: Repetition of key information followed by a check of accuracy. / contemporaneous note: A record made at or near the time of the event. / follow-up instruction: Direction about the next action from the appropriate person.
Speaking task: Read Luis and Jo aloud, then swap roles. Keep the client's tiredness report separate from the two observed pauses. Correct the ten gaps. Distinguish nine twenty as observation time from an unknown onset and unmeasured signs from normal results. Repeat the request for instructions and read-back. Preserve the urgent route without turning receipt of the report into clinical clearance.
Partner: Luis: Caregiver / Jo: Nurse receiving the report
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: At 14:10, a client says, "My usual shoes feel tighter today." The caregiver notices the client trying twice to fasten them. No cause or measurements are established; the local reporting route applies.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Luis: Please give me the next steps one at a time. I will repeat them back; if I miss a word, I want to check it now.
Jo: That read-back confirmation is helpful. Ask immediately if a direction, responsibility, or timing is unclear, and keep any action within the responsibilities you are authorized to perform.
Luis: My record will include her words, the two pauses, nine twenty, what I do not know, and this contact. It will not say that a cause has been confirmed.
Jo: Good. Make a contemporaneous note and record the actual instructions and response, not an expected outcome. Continue to use the appropriate local route if the concern changes.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Boundaries around medicines and clinical questionsA missed dose needs qualified advice+

Your goal

Explain a reminder-only role, acknowledge a medication question, and help obtain qualified advice without recommending a dose.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

A missed dose needs qualified advice

Mr. Ahmed tells caregiver Rosa that he missed a medicine dose and asks whether to take twice as much now. Rosa does not know the medicine, strength, prescribed schedule, or when the missed dose was due. Her fictional assignment permits reminders only, not administering medicines or recommending doses. The care plan names a nurse contact for medication questions. Rosa should explain her role and help seek timely qualified advice through the plan. She must not guess a dose, turn a missed dose into a refusal, or delay an urgent response for routine contact.

Mr. Ahmed: Client / Rosa: Caregiver

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does Rosa's assignment permit?

Answer and explanation

D. Reminders only

The fictional assignment expressly limits Rosa to reminders and excludes administration and dose recommendations.

2. What information is missing?

Answer and explanation

D. Medicine details and the relevant dose timing

The medicine, strength, schedule, and missed-dose timing are unknown in the brief.

3. What is the appropriate communication response?

Answer and explanation

A. Explain the limit and help contact the qualified advice route in the plan.

Qualified, medicine-specific advice is needed; neither a doubling nor a skipping rule can be invented.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Reminding, administering, and recommending a dose are different actions. The fictional role permits only the first. Recognizing a label or medicine name does not supply clinical authority, and another worker may have a different authorized role.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
medication reminder
A prompt within the agreed role to support a person's own medicine routine.give an authorized medication reminder
administration
Giving or applying medicine under the applicable authorization and competence requirements.distinguish administration from a reminder
prescriber
A professional authorized to prescribe the relevant medicine.contact the prescriber
pharmacist
A qualified medicines professional.seek pharmacist advice
prescription
An authorized instruction for a specified medicine.check the current prescription
dose
The amount of medicine specified for one administration.clarify the prescribed dose
strength
The amount of active ingredient per stated unit or quantity.identify the medicine strength
dosage form
The physical form of a medicine, such as a tablet or liquid.identify the dosage form
route of administration
How a medicine is taken or applied.confirm the route of administration
missed dose
A scheduled dose that was not taken at the intended time.report a missed dose
duplicate dose
An additional dose taken or given when the relevant dose has already been taken or given.report a possible duplicate dose
dosing interval
The time between doses under the prescribed schedule.clarify the dosing interval
dispensing label
The pharmacy label identifying the medicine and directions.refer to the dispensing label
medication list
A record of medicines whose accuracy and currency require confirmation.verify the medication list
MAR
Medication administration record; may record different types of medicines support under the service's process.record the actual support on the MAR
PRN
From pro re nata; medicine prescribed for use when needed under specified directions.clarify PRN directions
contraindication
A reason a particular treatment may be inappropriate in a given situation.refer a contraindication question
interaction
An effect arising from combining medicines or other relevant substances.ask about an interaction
side effect
An effect of a medicine other than its intended main action.report a suspected side effect
adverse reaction
An unwanted harmful response associated with a medicine.report a suspected adverse reaction
medication discrepancy
A difference between medicine information or records that needs clarification.report a medication discrepancy
clinical question
A question requiring relevant professional assessment or advice.route a clinical question
delegated task
A task formally assigned under the applicable authority and competence arrangements.clarify a delegated task
out-of-hours contact
The designated advice or response route outside ordinary service hours.use the out-of-hours contact

Missed, declined, and already taken describe different events. A MAR may record reminders and other medicines support as well as administration. Use the correct status under the service's process; do not record medicine given when you only made a call or gave a reminder.

C Notice the language

A helpful limit is more than a refusal

People may ask a trusted worker for an immediate answer. Acknowledge the concern, make the professional boundary understandable, and offer the approved route to advice. Avoid either guessing to seem helpful or leaving the person with only a curt disclaimer.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the question
I understand you want to know what to do about the missed dose.
State the role
My assignment permits reminders, not dose advice.
Separate the actions
A reminder is different from administering the medicine.
Decline to guess
I cannot safely decide that from the information I have.
Offer useful help
I can help you contact the nurse listed in your care plan.
Preserve the question
You are asking whether to take twice the usual amount.
Identify missing details
The professional will need the medicine details and relevant timing.
Avoid a blanket rule
Different medicines require different advice.
Ask about the contact route
Shall we use the nurse contact in the plan now?
Keep the client involved
You can explain what happened, and I can help communicate the question.
Clarify an unclear direction
Please repeat that instruction so we can check it accurately.
Separate reported facts
You told me the dose was missed; I did not observe when it was due.
Avoid false recording
I will not record a medicine as administered when I did not administer it.
Use the proper backup
If the usual contact is unavailable, we need the approved alternative route.
Keep urgency clear
An urgent concern should not wait for a routine callback.
Record the response
I will document the question, contact, and actual advice received through the required process.
6 language and precision notes
Permits reminders
Names the specific assignment boundary without claiming every caregiver has the same role.
Cannot decide
Avoids giving an unsupported answer disguised as a suggestion.
Listed in your plan
Directs the person to the actual agreed route rather than an invented service.
Relevant timing
Includes when the dose was due and what actually happened, if known.
Different medicines
Explains why a remembered general rule is inadequate.
Actual advice received
Keeps pending contact separate from a completed professional response.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response stays within the supplied role?

Answer and explanation

A. I cannot recommend a dose; I can help contact the nurse in your plan.

The response gives a useful qualified route without exceeding the reminder-only assignment.

2. Which record is inaccurate?

Answer and explanation

C. Dose administered by Rosa, when she only helped place a call

Helping obtain advice is not administration and must not be recorded as such.

3. What would justify a universal missed-dose rule here?

Answer and explanation

A. Nothing in the supplied facts

The medicine and circumstances are unknown, and clinical advice is outside the assigned role.

4. What if the usual nurse contact is unavailable?

Answer and explanation

C. Use the approved alternative or urgent route appropriate to the concern.

The appropriate backup pathway preserves timely access to advice without inventing medicine-specific instructions.

E Conversations

A missed dose needs qualified advice

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Mr. Ahmed: Client / Rosa: Caregiver

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • follow-up instruction
  • dosing interval
  • missed dose
  • prescriber
  • strength
  • Administration
  • out-of-hours contact
  • clinical question
  • medication discrepancy
  • medication reminder
  1. Mr. Ahmed
    Rosa, I missed a dose of my medicine. Should I take twice as much now to make up for it? I do not want to get behind.
  2. Rosa
    That is a 1, and I cannot decide the dose. My assignment covers reminders only, but I can help you reach the nurse named in your care plan.
  3. Mr. Ahmed
    You usually ask whether I have remembered my medicine. I thought that meant you could tell me how to fix a missed one as well.
  4. Rosa
    A 2 and advice about changing a dose are different. I can prompt within the agreed plan; I cannot choose a new amount or recommend how to replace a missed dose.
  5. Mr. Ahmed
    The medicine is in the other room. I do not remember the exact name, and I have not told you when I was supposed to take it.
  6. Rosa
    Those details matter, including the name, 3, and timing. We should give the professional accurate information, not rely on a tablet's color.
  7. Mr. Ahmed
    I read somewhere that people sometimes take the next dose as usual. Could we just follow that advice and avoid bothering the nurse?
  8. Rosa
    I cannot turn that into a rule for your 4. The right response depends on the medicine and circumstances, and those have not been established in our conversation.
  9. Mr. Ahmed
    All right. The care plan has the number we normally use. I would like help making the call because I sometimes lose track of the question.
  10. Rosa
    I can help you reach that contact and explain the question clearly. The 5 or another appropriate medicines professional may need to advise through the agreed route.
  11. Mr. Ahmed
    Please say I missed it. I did not decide against taking it, and I do not want the nurse to hear that I refused.
  12. Rosa
    I will preserve that distinction in the 6 report if the process requires one. I will describe what you told me without adding a motive or an administration event I did not observe.
  13. Mr. Ahmed
    If the nurse tells us something I do not understand, can you ask them to repeat it while I am still on the line?
  14. Rosa
    Yes. We can ask about the 7 or any other unclear wording, and repeat the instructions back. Clarifying professional advice is different from making up the advice ourselves.
  15. Mr. Ahmed
    What if nobody answers? Can we check the backup contact now rather than wait without a plan?
  16. Rosa
    We should use the approved 8 or other backup route when applicable. If there is an urgent or emergency concern, we must follow that route without waiting for a routine callback.
  17. Mr. Ahmed
    Please do not mark medicine taken just because we discussed it or tried to call.
  18. Rosa
    I will not. 9 has not happened through me. My note will describe the reported missed dose, the contact attempt, and any actual response, according to the recording procedure.
  19. Mr. Ahmed
    Thank you for explaining the difference. Let us contact the nurse now, with the question and the medicine information ready for them.
  20. Rosa
    We will follow the plan and clarify any 10 we receive. Until qualified advice is obtained, I will not give you a guessed answer about doubling or replacing the dose.

Answers and explanations
  1. clinical question Turn 2. The dose decision needs qualified, medicine-specific advice rather than a caregiver's guess.
  2. medication reminder Turn 4. A medication reminder supports the agreed routine but does not authorize dose changes or administration.
  3. strength Turn 6. Strength identifies the amount of active ingredient per unit and is one relevant detail for qualified advice.
  4. missed dose Turn 8. A missed dose requires advice appropriate to the actual medicine and circumstances, which are not established here.
  5. prescriber Turn 10. The prescriber is an authorized clinical source; the actual advice route in this plan begins with the named nurse contact.
  6. medication discrepancy Turn 12. The medication discrepancy concerns the reported missed dose and relevant records, not an assumed deliberate refusal.
  7. dosing interval Turn 14. The dosing interval is the time between doses; unclear professional directions should be clarified rather than guessed.
  8. out-of-hours contact Turn 16. The out-of-hours contact is one possible designated alternative; the actual plan determines which backup route applies.
  9. Administration Turn 18. Administration is the actual giving or application of medicine under the appropriate role, not discussion or a telephone attempt.
  10. follow-up instruction Turn 20. A follow-up instruction must come from the appropriate professional and be understood accurately before it is recorded or followed.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Mr. Ahmed: Rosa, I missed a dose of my medicine. Should I take twice as much now to make up for it? I do not want to get behind.
  2. Rosa: That is a clinical question, and I cannot decide the dose. My assignment covers reminders only, but I can help you reach the nurse named in your care plan.
  3. Mr. Ahmed: You usually ask whether I have remembered my medicine. I thought that meant you could tell me how to fix a missed one as well.
  4. Rosa: A medication reminder and advice about changing a dose are different. I can prompt within the agreed plan; I cannot choose a new amount or recommend how to replace a missed dose.
  5. Mr. Ahmed: The medicine is in the other room. I do not remember the exact name, and I have not told you when I was supposed to take it.
  6. Rosa: Those details matter, including the name, strength, and timing. We should give the professional accurate information, not rely on a tablet's color.
  7. Mr. Ahmed: I read somewhere that people sometimes take the next dose as usual. Could we just follow that advice and avoid bothering the nurse?
  8. Rosa: I cannot turn that into a rule for your missed dose. The right response depends on the medicine and circumstances, and those have not been established in our conversation.
  9. Mr. Ahmed: All right. The care plan has the number we normally use. I would like help making the call because I sometimes lose track of the question.
  10. Rosa: I can help you reach that contact and explain the question clearly. The prescriber or another appropriate medicines professional may need to advise through the agreed route.
  11. Mr. Ahmed: Please say I missed it. I did not decide against taking it, and I do not want the nurse to hear that I refused.
  12. Rosa: I will preserve that distinction in the medication discrepancy report if the process requires one. I will describe what you told me without adding a motive or an administration event I did not observe.
  13. Mr. Ahmed: If the nurse tells us something I do not understand, can you ask them to repeat it while I am still on the line?
  14. Rosa: Yes. We can ask about the dosing interval or any other unclear wording, and repeat the instructions back. Clarifying professional advice is different from making up the advice ourselves.
  15. Mr. Ahmed: What if nobody answers? Can we check the backup contact now rather than wait without a plan?
  16. Rosa: We should use the approved out-of-hours contact or other backup route when applicable. If there is an urgent or emergency concern, we must follow that route without waiting for a routine callback.
  17. Mr. Ahmed: Please do not mark medicine taken just because we discussed it or tried to call.
  18. Rosa: I will not. Administration has not happened through me. My note will describe the reported missed dose, the contact attempt, and any actual response, according to the recording procedure.
  19. Mr. Ahmed: Thank you for explaining the difference. Let us contact the nurse now, with the question and the medicine information ready for them.
  20. Rosa: We will follow the plan and clarify any follow-up instruction we receive. Until qualified advice is obtained, I will not give you a guessed answer about doubling or replacing the dose.
Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Please tell me which dose

The caregiver responds to the fictional missed-dose request without offering medical advice.

  1. Client: I missed yesterday's medicine. Should I take twice as much today to make up for it?
  2. Caregiver: I understand why you're asking, but I cannot recommend a dose or tell you to double it.
  3. Client: You remind me about medicines, so I thought you would know what to do.
  4. Caregiver: A medication reminder is part of my assignment. Choosing a dose is outside my scope of duties.
  5. Client: I don't want to spend the whole morning trying to reach the right person.
  6. Caregiver: The care plan gives a nurse contact for these questions. May I help you contact that person?
  7. Client: Yes. What should I say when we reach the nurse on the phone?
  8. Caregiver: Explain that you missed a dose and ask for their guidance. I can help communicate the question.
  9. Client: So you aren't saying the answer is yes or no to taking extra?
  10. Caregiver: Correct. I'm saying that decision needs authorized clinical advice, and I won't guess about it.
A relative's new instruction

A caregiver discusses an unverified change to an authorized task with the coordinator.

  1. Caregiver: The client's daughter says my duties now include administering medicine, but my current assignment permits reminders only.
  2. Coordinator: Have you received an updated plan or confirmation from the authorized person responsible for that change?
  3. Caregiver: No. The only new information is the daughter's statement during today's visit at nine thirty.
  4. Coordinator: Then don't treat that statement alone as a change to your authorized duties. I'll check the plan.
  5. Caregiver: I can continue the agreed nonclinical tasks while you clarify, but I won't perform the additional task.
  6. Coordinator: That's the correct distinction. Tell the client we are checking the arrangement, not abandoning the request.
  7. Caregiver: Should I explain that the question is about authorization, not whether I trust the family member?
  8. Coordinator: Yes. Keep the language factual and avoid implying that she deliberately gave you incorrect information.
  9. Caregiver: I'll say the current assignment and the new request differ, and that we need an authorized clarification.
  10. Coordinator: I'll contact the responsible person now and confirm the outcome through the approved communication channel.
The neighbor said it was fine

A client asks the caregiver to endorse advice received from a neighbor.

  1. Client: My neighbor takes something similar and says I can change the timing. Is that all right?
  2. Caregiver: I can't confirm that advice. Similar medicines or situations may still involve different instructions for different people.
  3. Client: But she has been taking it for years and sounded very sure when we spoke.
  4. Caregiver: I hear that she was confident. Her experience doesn't give me authority to advise on your medicine.
  5. Client: I just want someone to tell me whether her suggestion makes sense for me.
  6. Caregiver: That question belongs with an appropriately qualified professional. We can use the contact route in your plan.
  7. Client: Can you explain what she suggested when we call, so I don't forget the details?
  8. Caregiver: I can help relay your question, making clear that it came from a neighbor and is not an instruction.
  9. Client: All right. Please don't describe it as something I have already decided to do.
  10. Caregiver: I won't. I'll distinguish advice you heard from a decision or action you have actually taken.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A missed dose needs qualified advice
  1. Read Mr. Ahmed and Rosa aloud, then swap roles. State the reminder-only assignment and offer help contacting the named nurse.
  2. Correct the ten gaps. Preserve the missed-dose question without recommending doubling, skipping, or any other dose change.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Route a question about a changed label

A reminder-only worker notices that the client reports different directions on a new dispensing label. The worker has not verified the difference. The plan names the supplying pharmacy as the advice contact.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Worker: "You are reporting a possible medication ___."

Answer and explanation

D. discrepancy

The reported difference between directions requires clarification, not an assumed corrected dose.

2. Client: "Please help me contact the supplying ___."

Answer and explanation

A. pharmacy

The supplied plan specifically names the supplying pharmacy as the advice contact.

3. Worker: "My assigned support is limited to ___."

Answer and explanation

A. reminders

The fictional role allows reminders rather than dose decisions or administration.

4. Worker: "The reported difference is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

B. verified

The worker has not confirmed the reported difference and should preserve that uncertainty.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Course: Home Care & Caregiving English
Professional audience: Home care aides, personal care assistants, and caregivers
Scope: People, schedules, care plans, and incidents are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not clinical care, medication instructions, capacity assessment, or professional certification. Follow the actual care plan, consent and privacy requirements, training, employer procedures, and qualified clinical advice. For urgent or emergency concerns use the appropriate local service and procedure without waiting for a routine callback. The cited England/UK sources do not establish universal rules.
Lesson 04: Boundaries around medicines and clinical questions
Original fictional case: Mr. Ahmed tells caregiver Rosa that he missed a medicine dose and asks whether to take twice as much now. Rosa does not know the medicine, strength, prescribed schedule, or when the missed dose was due. Her fictional assignment permits reminders only, not administering medicines or recommending doses. The care plan names a nurse contact for medication questions. Rosa should explain her role and help seek timely qualified advice through the plan. She must not guess a dose, turn a missed dose into a refusal, or delay an urgent response for routine contact.
Language workshop: A missed dose needs qualified advice
Communication goal: Explain a reminder-only role, acknowledge a medication question, and help obtain qualified advice without recommending a dose.
Language guidance: Reminding, administering, and recommending a dose are different actions. The fictional role permits only the first. Recognizing a label or medicine name does not supply clinical authority, and another worker may have a different authorized role.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand you want to know what to do about the missed dose. / My assignment permits reminders, not dose advice. / A reminder is different from administering the medicine. / I cannot safely decide that from the information I have. / I can help you contact the nurse listed in your care plan. / You are asking whether to take twice the usual amount. / The professional will need the medicine details and relevant timing. / Different medicines require different advice. / Shall we use the nurse contact in the plan now? / You can explain what happened, and I can help communicate the question. / Please repeat that instruction so we can check it accurately. / You told me the dose was missed; I did not observe when it was due. / I will not record a medicine as administered when I did not administer it. / If the usual contact is unavailable, we need the approved alternative route. / An urgent concern should not wait for a routine callback. / I will document the question, contact, and actual advice received through the required process.
Vocabulary: medication reminder: A prompt within the agreed role to support a person's own medicine routine. / administration: Giving or applying medicine under the applicable authorization and competence requirements. / prescriber: A professional authorized to prescribe the relevant medicine. / pharmacist: A qualified medicines professional. / prescription: An authorized instruction for a specified medicine. / dose: The amount of medicine specified for one administration. / strength: The amount of active ingredient per stated unit or quantity. / dosage form: The physical form of a medicine, such as a tablet or liquid. / route of administration: How a medicine is taken or applied. / missed dose: A scheduled dose that was not taken at the intended time. / duplicate dose: An additional dose taken or given when the relevant dose has already been taken or given. / dosing interval: The time between doses under the prescribed schedule. / dispensing label: The pharmacy label identifying the medicine and directions. / medication list: A record of medicines whose accuracy and currency require confirmation. / MAR: Medication administration record; may record different types of medicines support under the service's process. / PRN: From pro re nata; medicine prescribed for use when needed under specified directions. / contraindication: A reason a particular treatment may be inappropriate in a given situation. / interaction: An effect arising from combining medicines or other relevant substances. / side effect: An effect of a medicine other than its intended main action. / adverse reaction: An unwanted harmful response associated with a medicine. / medication discrepancy: A difference between medicine information or records that needs clarification. / clinical question: A question requiring relevant professional assessment or advice. / delegated task: A task formally assigned under the applicable authority and competence arrangements. / out-of-hours contact: The designated advice or response route outside ordinary service hours.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Language guidance: Reminding, administering, and recommending a dose are different actions. The fictional role permits only the first. Recognizing a label or medicine name does not supply clinical authority, and another worker may have a different authorized role.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand you want to know what to do about the missed dose. / My assignment permits reminders, not dose advice. / A reminder is different from administering the medicine. / I cannot safely decide that from the information I have. / I can help you contact the nurse listed in your care plan. / You are asking whether to take twice the usual amount. / The professional will need the medicine details and relevant timing. / Different medicines require different advice. / Shall we use the nurse contact in the plan now? / You can explain what happened, and I can help communicate the question. / Please repeat that instruction so we can check it accurately. / You told me the dose was missed; I did not observe when it was due. / I will not record a medicine as administered when I did not administer it. / If the usual contact is unavailable, we need the approved alternative route. / An urgent concern should not wait for a routine callback. / I will document the question, contact, and actual advice received through the required process.
Vocabulary: medication reminder: A prompt within the agreed role to support a person's own medicine routine. / administration: Giving or applying medicine under the applicable authorization and competence requirements. / prescriber: A professional authorized to prescribe the relevant medicine. / pharmacist: A qualified medicines professional. / prescription: An authorized instruction for a specified medicine. / dose: The amount of medicine specified for one administration. / strength: The amount of active ingredient per stated unit or quantity. / dosage form: The physical form of a medicine, such as a tablet or liquid. / route of administration: How a medicine is taken or applied. / missed dose: A scheduled dose that was not taken at the intended time. / duplicate dose: An additional dose taken or given when the relevant dose has already been taken or given. / dosing interval: The time between doses under the prescribed schedule. / dispensing label: The pharmacy label identifying the medicine and directions. / medication list: A record of medicines whose accuracy and currency require confirmation. / MAR: Medication administration record; may record different types of medicines support under the service's process. / PRN: From pro re nata; medicine prescribed for use when needed under specified directions. / contraindication: A reason a particular treatment may be inappropriate in a given situation. / interaction: An effect arising from combining medicines or other relevant substances. / side effect: An effect of a medicine other than its intended main action. / adverse reaction: An unwanted harmful response associated with a medicine. / medication discrepancy: A difference between medicine information or records that needs clarification. / clinical question: A question requiring relevant professional assessment or advice. / delegated task: A task formally assigned under the applicable authority and competence arrangements. / out-of-hours contact: The designated advice or response route outside ordinary service hours.
Speaking task: Read Mr. Ahmed and Rosa aloud, then swap roles. State the reminder-only assignment and offer help contacting the named nurse. Correct the ten gaps. Preserve the missed-dose question without recommending doubling, skipping, or any other dose change. Repeat the backup-contact and recording exchanges. A call, a reminder, and actual medicine administration must retain their different statuses.
Partner: Mr. Ahmed: Client / Rosa: Caregiver
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A reminder-only worker notices that the client reports different directions on a new dispensing label. The worker has not verified the difference. The plan names the supplying pharmacy as the advice contact.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Mr. Ahmed: Please do not mark medicine taken just because we discussed it or tried to call.
Rosa: I will not. Administration has not happened through me. My note will describe the reported missed dose, the contact attempt, and any actual response, according to the recording procedure.
Mr. Ahmed: Thank you for explaining the difference. Let us contact the nurse now, with the question and the medicine information ready for them.
Rosa: We will follow the plan and clarify any follow-up instruction we receive. Until qualified advice is obtained, I will not give you a guessed answer about doubling or replacing the dose.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Home Care & Caregiving English
Professional audience: Home care aides, personal care assistants, and caregivers
Scope: People, schedules, care plans, and incidents are fictional. This book teaches workplace English, not clinical care, medication instructions, capacity assessment, or professional certification. Follow the actual care plan, consent and privacy requirements, training, employer procedures, and qualified clinical advice. For urgent or emergency concerns use the appropriate local service and procedure without waiting for a routine callback. The cited England/UK sources do not establish universal rules.
Lesson 04: Boundaries around medicines and clinical questions
Original fictional case: Mr. Ahmed tells caregiver Rosa that he missed a medicine dose and asks whether to take twice as much now. Rosa does not know the medicine, strength, prescribed schedule, or when the missed dose was due. Her fictional assignment permits reminders only, not administering medicines or recommending doses. The care plan names a nurse contact for medication questions. Rosa should explain her role and help seek timely qualified advice through the plan. She must not guess a dose, turn a missed dose into a refusal, or delay an urgent response for routine contact.
Language workshop: A missed dose needs qualified advice
Communication goal: Explain a reminder-only role, acknowledge a medication question, and help obtain qualified advice without recommending a dose.
